## Assistant

`E:\IdeaProjects\som-knowledge-machine` stays the working directory. The port will follow the Lisp’s own module boundaries, one piece at a time, and each finished piece will get a plain-English write-up of what it now does.

The source is Knowledge Machine 2.5.45, one concatenated file of about 26,800 lines under the Simplified BSD License (Peter Clark and Bruce Porter, 1994–2015). It is a frame-and-slot reasoner: you load a knowledge base, then ask questions such as `(the slot of frame)`. The file is already marked into the original modules.

## How each step will be recorded

Each finished step adds `docs/migration/NN-title.md` and a line in `docs/migration/README.md`. The note will say:

- what the piece is
- what you can do after this step that you could not do before
- which Lisp files it comes from
- what is still absent
- how to try it

The original license notice stays with the port.

## Order

Later steps depend on earlier ones. Global flags in `header.lisp` arrive with the piece that uses them.

| Step | Piece | Lisp source | Result |
|---|---|---|---|
| 0 | Clojure project shell | — | `deps.edn`, `som.km` namespaces, tests, license, migration index |
| 1 | Read and print KM terms | `case.lisp` | KM text becomes Clojure data and prints back, including `#$` forms and preserved case |
| 2 | Pure helpers | `strings.lisp`, `minimatch.lisp`, the KB-free parts of `utils.lisp` and `kbutils.lisp` | Pattern match and symbol/string helpers, tested on their own |
| 3 | Knowledge base | `frame-io.lisp` | Frames, slots, situations, inverses, assert, retract, lookup. Memory without inference |
| 4 | Load a KB | `loadkb.lisp`, `initkb.lisp` | A `.km` file loads, including the built-in classes |
| 5 | Taxonomy | `subsumes.lisp`, `taxonomy.lisp` | Class hierarchy: supers, subclasses, subsumption, printed tree |
| 6 | Goal stack | `stack.lisp` | Call stack and loop detection, required before queries call themselves |
| 7 | Interpreter | `interpreter.lisp` | Query in, value out. Operators that need no inheritance first; each later batch named in the note |
| 8 | Slot values | `get-slotvals.lisp` | `(the slot of X)` follows inheritance |
| 9 | Unification | `lazy-unify.lisp` | `&` and instance merging |
| 10 | Constraints | `constraints.lisp` | `must-be-a`, `exactly`, `at-least`, `at-most` |
| 11 | Prototypes | `prototypes.lisp` | Prototype graphs and cloning |
| 12 | Explanations | `explain.lisp` | Why an answer was produced |
| 13 | English and frames | `anglify.lisp`, `writer.lisp` | Text fragments and `write-frame` |
| 14 | Surface | `trace.lisp`, `stats.lisp`, `sadl.lisp`, `licence.lisp` | Trace, statistics, the SADL event-chain walker, license text, and the `KM>` prompt |

`compiled-handlers.lisp` and `compiler.lisp` are a generated flattening of the same interpreter handlers, kept in Lisp for speed. The Clojure port keeps a single handler implementation. `htextify.lisp` is a stub and will not be ported.

The knowledge base stays mutable. Unification rewrites frames in place, and situations share that store, so the port will use Clojure atoms (or refs) around the frame store rather than pretending each query returns a new database.

Say when to start with step 0, or name a different first piece.

## Assistant

Step 0 is in place. The repository is a Clojure project with a `KM>` prompt and an HTTP service, and both call `som.km.engine`. That engine accepts text and does not evaluate it yet.

The prompt and the service are adapters. `evaluate` returns one map: `:ok`, `:error`, `:message`, and `:input`. The prompt prints the message. The service returns the same map as JSON. There is no knowledge base yet, so the two fronts have nothing to share. When the knowledge base arrives, each front will hold its own value.

- `clojure -M:repl` prints the Knowledge Machine 2.5.45 banner. A line is answered with a refusal. `q` leaves the prompt, as in the Lisp read-eval-print loop. `Q` does not.
- `clojure -M:server` listens on `http://127.0.0.1:8080`. `PORT` chooses another port.
- `GET /status` returns the build, including both interfaces.
- `POST /query` takes the expression as plain text. `(the age of Fred)` returns 501 and the same refusal the prompt prints. A blank body returns 400. A JSON body returns 415. An unknown path returns 404. Sending `q` does not stop the service.

The write-up is in `docs/migration/00-project-shell.md`, with the index in `docs/migration/README.md`. The original Simplified BSD Licence is in `LICENSE`.

`clojure -M:test` ran 16 tests, 43 assertions, with no failures. A live prompt accepted `(a Cat)` and left on `q`. A live server on port 18081 returned the 501 and 400 bodies above. An invalid `PORT` exits with a message.

The HTTP server is the one in the JDK. Ring is on Clojars, and this machine's Nexus mirror serves Maven Central, so a Ring dependency does not resolve. The handler is a plain function, and a later step can put another server in front of it without changing the routes or the engine.

The Clojure CLI was installed so the project can run. `clojure` is on your user PATH at `C:\Users\scott\AppData\Local\Apps\clojure\`. A new terminal will see it.

Next is step 1: read a Knowledge Machine expression and print it back.

## Assistant

`AGENTS.md` is at the project root. It has the four sections you named.

**Project Direction.** The port stays faithful to Knowledge Machine 2.5.45, with the Lisp file as the specification. The engine is a library with explicit state, so the prompt and the HTTP service can each hold a knowledge base. The work proceeds one Lisp module at a time, with one implementation of each behavior.

**Reference.** Lasting design notes live in `docs/reference/`. The section points at `docs/reference/Architecture.md`, which records the engine, the two adapters, the result map, and where state will live. Step history stays in `docs/migration/`.

**Coding Conventions.** Functions take values and return values. Effects stay in the prompt and the HTTP service. The knowledge base is the one piece of state, passed in by the caller. Lazy sequences stay away from updates to that store. Results are maps, and tests hit the engine and each adapter.

**Change Discipline.** One migration step at a time, with a plain-English note, an architecture update when the structure changes, tests, and `clojure -M:test` before the step is done. A new engine behavior is reachable from both fronts through the same function.

## Assistant

The Perl unpacker is extracted and has been run. The packaged Knowledge Machine source is now 30 separate files in `src/knowledge-machine/`.

The script is `src/knowledge-machine/disassemble`, copied from the cut-out block in `km-2-5-45-packaged.lisp`. It read 29,613 lines and split on each `;;; FILE:` header. The packaged file is unchanged.

Every file except `README.txt` starts with the two forms that were already active in the script:

```lisp
(unless (find-package :km) (make-package :km :use '(:common-lisp)))
(in-package :km)
```

So each Lisp file is in the `km` package. The files are `README.txt`, `loadme.lisp`, `header.lisp`, `htextify.lisp`, `case.lisp`, `interpreter.lisp`, `get-slotvals.lisp`, `frame-io.lisp`, `trace.lisp`, `explain.lisp`, `prototypes.lisp`, `lazy-unify.lisp`, `constraints.lisp`, `kbutils.lisp`, `stack.lisp`, `stats.lisp`, `sadl.lisp`, `utils.lisp`, `writer.lisp`, `taxonomy.lisp`, `subsumes.lisp`, `anglify.lisp`, `loadkb.lisp`, `minimatch.lisp`, `strings.lisp`, `compiler.lisp`, `compiled-handlers.lisp`, `licence.lisp`, `LICENCE`, and `initkb.lisp`.

The script's log shows a leading space in each name because `split` keeps the space after `FILE:`. Perl's two-argument `open` treats that space as the gap after `>`, so the files themselves are named `header.lisp`, `interpreter.lisp`, and so on.

## Assistant

The migration plan is in `docs/migration/plan.md`. The step list in `docs/migration/README.md` now follows it. `loadme.lisp` is the Lisp load order, and it is not the build order: `frame-io.lisp`, `loadkb.lisp`, the interpreter, unification, constraints, subsumption, prototypes, and explanations all call each other.

The port order breaks those cycles by writing pure code first and leaving a stub on each back edge.

1. Read and print terms, including comment and source syntax.
2. Patterns, lists, and `km-equal`.
3. The frame store. Calls back into the interpreter, subsumption, and the object stack stay stubbed.
4. Built-in vocabulary and `reset-kb`.
5. The goal stack.
6. The interpreter, in handler groups, through local slot lookup only.
7. Unification and constraints, together.
8. Subsumption.
9. Inheritance and subslots.
10. Prototypes.
11. Explanations.
12. Situations, actions, theories, projection, and supersituations.
13. Load, save, and undo. `fastsave` stays unported.
14. English text and the taxonomy listing.
15. Trace, statistics, SADL, and the license text.

`compiler.lisp` and `compiled-handlers.lisp` stay unported. `htextify.lisp` stays a function that returns nothing.

These are the Lisp behaviors that change an answer if Clojure's own rules are used:

- Lisp treats an empty list as failure. Clojure treats `()` and `[]` as true. The emptiness test is `km-null`. The symbol `nil` and Lisp's empty value are the same empty answer, and the Boolean instances are the symbols `t` and `f`.
- `km-equal` uses a tolerance of `0.0001` and refuses to treat two different symbols as equal. Clojure `=` does neither. `delete-duplicates` with `:from-end t` keeps the earlier duplicate. Ordinary slot values are appended, and taxonomy slots are inserted at the front, so `(the1 ...)` depends on that order.
- Arithmetic follows the rewrite rules, so `(2 + 3 * 4)` is `14` and `(2 - 3 - 4)` associates left. Integers stay exact, and `/` stays a ratio. `0/0` is `1`, `0` divided by a non-zero number is `0`, and a non-zero number divided by `0` is the integer `999999`.
- The reader is not `clojure.core/read`. Dotted tails are how `#,` splices. `#t` sees only a map the caller supplies, which is empty at the prompt and on HTTP. Skolem names come from `*km-gensym-counter*`, with prefixes `_`, `_Proto`, and `_Some`.
- `(km ...)` can return four values. The port returns one map with `:value`, `:errors`, `:error-structures`, and `:warnings`. `in-situation` restores the previous situation only on a normal return. A KM abort leaves the temporary situation in place. `debug` and `break` have no Lisp debugger. `eval`, `setq`, and `fastsave` of Common Lisp are refused.
- Three comments in the parameter list disagree with the bindings that actually run: `*sanity-checks*` is `nil`, `*tolerance*` is `0.0001`, and `*record-explanations-for-clones*` is `t`.

The reader port is not started. Say when to begin step 1.

At `KM>`, `(a Cat)` prints as `(a Cat)`, and the case of each name is kept. An unfinished line such as `(every Car has` waits for the rest. `q` leaves the prompt. `POST /query` returns status 200 and the same printed text. A blank body or a broken expression returns status 400. Posting `q` returns the symbol and does not stop the service.

A few Lisp details are recorded in that note:

- `minimatch` returns captured pieces in the order the variables are met. `(a ?x ?x)` against `(a b c)` returns `(b c)`. `full-match` records binding pairs. A repeated variable keeps both pairs, and `val-of` returns the earlier one.
- `km-equal` uses a tolerance of `0.0001`. Empty nil and the symbol `nil` match. `1` and `1.0` match under `km-equal`. They stay distinct under `equal` and `eql`.
- The default duplicate removal keeps the later copy. `:from-end? true` keeps the earlier copy. `remove-dup-instances` uses that earlier-copy rule and does not follow instance pointers.
- Symbols match by name. Each read builds a fresh symbol object.
- A cons cell is a record, so it answers `map?`. Handler lookup tests for a cons cell first.

## Assistant

Step 5 is finished. A query now keeps a goal stack and can tell when a goal repeats.

`som.km.stack` records each goal as five elements: the canonical expression, the situation, the expression that was pushed, the inference number, and the target. A unification is stored under `|all situations|`. `looping-on?` compares canonical forms after bindings are followed, so `(the parts of _X)` and `(the parts of _Engine1)` are the same goal once `_X` is bound. `&+!` stays distinct from `&`. An `or` between a goal and its repeat is still a loop. `handle-looping` returns the values already stored, `(t)` for `&?` and `&+?`, or nil. `reset` clears the goal stack and the object stack.

`clojure -M:test` from `E:\IdeaProjects\som-knowledge-machine` passed 114 tests and 913 assertions. The note is `docs/migration/05-the-goal-stack.md`. The migration index and `docs/reference/Architecture.md` include this step. `(show-goal-stack)` is still printed as text. Nothing is committed. Step 6 has not started.

Step 6 is finished. The prompt and the HTTP service both evaluate through one dispatcher, and a form this step does not run stays in the table and returns the not-ported refusal.

`(1 + 2)` answers `(3)`. Ratios, large integers, and the three division-by-zero results are the Lisp values, including for `0.0`. `(Fred has (color (red)))` stores the color, and `(the color of Fred)` answers `(red)`. `(assert (:triple Sue age 30))` is that same store. Constraint forms answer nil and set the flag. They are not enforced. `(reset-kb)`, the stack inspectors, and the fail-mode commands run. `eval`, `setq`, load and save, and trace are refused by name.

`:value` is the answer list. `:errors`, `:error-structures`, and `:warnings` carry the extra Lisp values. Each adapter holds its own knowledge base and query. Two requests on one running service see each other's assertions.

## Assistant

Step 7 is finished. Unification and local constraint checks run through the same engine the prompt and the HTTP service already use.

`&` and `&!` combine two values and keep the result. `&&` and `&+` merge a pair when the classes stored so far subsume. A pair that fails that test stays in the `&&` result. `&?` and `&+?` only test, and the trial is discarded. A lazy result may leave `(_A & _B)` on a slot. The next read of that slot finishes it. A constraint stored with a value is checked on that read. A constraint form by itself still answers nil. `(a Cat)` is still refused.

`clojure -M:test` passed 143 tests and 1123 assertions, with 0 failures. On a live server, `(1 & 1)` is HTTP 200 and the value is `[1]`. `(a Cat)` stays HTTP 501.

The note is `docs/migration/07-unification-and-constraints.md`. The differences from the Lisp are recorded there, including that `&&` does not create `(a Class)` and does not drop a value an existential covers, and that Thing is not invented as a superclass. Step 8 has not been started.

## Assistant

Step 8 is done. An existing instance can be tested against a description, and a value that description already covers is not stored again.

`(_Cat1 is '(a Cat with (color (red))))` answers `(t)` when that cat is red. `(the Cat with (color (red)))` answers `(_Cat1)`. `(every Cat)` answers the stored cats, newest first. A miss records that no values were found and adds no frame. Two matches record an error and answer nil.

`(the+ Cat with ...)` and `(a+ Cat with ...)` return a match that is already stored. A miss is still refused. `(a Cat)` and `(every Cat has ...)` stay refused, including over HTTP. On one live service, `(the Cat with (color (red)))` is HTTP 200 and the value is `[{"symbol":"_Cat1"}]`, and the following `(a Cat)` is still HTTP 501.

`((_Cat1) && ((a Cat)))` answers `(_Cat1)` and creates no Skolem. The same check with `(a Cat with (color (red)))` stores red. `(Number subsumes Integer)` answers `(t)` from the built-in subclass link. `(every (airplane parts Wing))` finds a wing whose `parts-of` is `airplane`.

`(the ...)` searches and does not create. `(the+ ...)` searches, then the create is refused. The candidate set is the object stack. The inverse read `(the Class invslot of value)` is discarded. A description with slots is still refused and leaves nothing behind. `(every Thing)` answers `(Cat)`, the class frame, and does not answer `_Cat1`, because Cat has no stored path to Thing.

`clojure -M:test` passed 156 tests and 1195 assertions. The note is `docs/migration/08-subsumption.md`. Step 9 has not started.

## Assistant

Step 9 is done. A slot read now uses the local store, the rules inherited from classes, and the values on subslots. `clojure -M:test` passed 165 tests and 1260 assertions.

`(every Cat has (color (black)))` answers `(Cat)`. An instance of Cat then answers `(black)` for `(the color of _Cat1)`. `(the color of Cat)` stays nil, because that rule belongs to members of Cat. `(every Cat also-has ...)` adds a value, and `(every Cat now-has ...)` replaces the class rule for an instance that has not been read yet. A finished read writes its values on the instance, so a later `now-has` combines those stored values with the new rule.

`(the all-superclasses of Integer)` is `(Thing Number)`. `(the all-classes of 1)` is `(Thing Integer Number)`. `(the subclasses of Sequence)` stays the stored `(Triple Pair)`. `(color has (subslots (hue)))` makes a hue value show up as a color. An inherited `(must-be-a Color)` classifies an unclassified value. An inherited `(mustnt-be-a Dog)` records a violation and keeps the value.

`(a Cat)`, `(every Cat has-definition ...)`, and `(why)` stay refused. The note is `docs/migration/09-inheritance-and-subslots.md`. Step 10 has not been started.

import pathlib
p = pathlib.Path(r'E:\IdeaProjects\som-knowledge-machine\src\som\km\situations.clj')
text = p.read_text(encoding='utf-8')
lines = text.splitlines()
# track paren depth ignoring strings and comments, report depth at each defn and when depth goes negative
depth = 0
in_str = False
escape = False
in_comment = False
start_line = None
for i, line in enumerate(lines, 1):
    j = 0
    while j < len(line):
        c = line[j]
        if in_comment:
            break
        if in_str:
            if escape:
                escape = False
            elif c == '\\\\':
                escape = True
            elif c == '\"':
                in_str = False
            j += 1
            continue
        if c == ';':
            in_comment = True
            break
        if c == '\"':
            in_str = True
        elif c == '(':
            if depth == 0:
                start_line = i
            depth += 1
        elif c == ')':
            depth -= 1
            if depth == 0:
                pass
            if depth < 0:
                print('NEG', i, j, line)
                depth = 0
        j += 1
    in_comment = False
    if 890 <= i <= 1000 and (i % 10 == 0 or i in (901,903,990,991)):
        print(f'{i}: depth={depth} start={start_line}')
print('FINAL', depth, 'in_str', in_str)

Step 12 is in place. `clojure -M:test` from `E:\IdeaProjects\som-knowledge-machine` passed 196 tests and 1446 assertions.

A knowledge base can leave `*Global`. `(new-situation)` enters `_Situation1` and turns situations mode on. A body entered from another situation restores the previous one when it returns. `(a Situation)` and `(a Theory)` create an instance. `(a Cat)` stays refused. An inertial fluent projects from the previous situation, and the frame records `(projected-from _Situation1)`. `(why)` follows that record into the previous situation. Own facts climb supersituations and visible theories. `(do _Move)` asserts its `add-list` in the next situation and returns to the old one. The prompt shows a local situation in brackets and a theory in braces.

A read while a theory is visible copies that fluent onto the instance. Hiding the theory leaves the copy. A fluent in that theory that was not read is absent afterward.

The note is `docs/migration/12-situations.md`. The README done list includes step 12, and what is still ahead starts at load, save, and undo. Nothing is committed.

## Assistant

Step 13 is done. A knowledge base saves as KM text and loads back, and undo restores a snapshot of the value the caller holds. `clojure -M:test` passed 212 tests and 1519 assertions.

`(start-logging)` records a snapshot before each top-level command except `showme`, `undo`, and `why`. `(Fred has (color (red)))`, then `(undo)`, clears that color and prints the undone expression. `(checkpoint here)` and `(undo here)` return to the named snapshot. `(keeping-kb ...)` runs the body and puts the frames back. `(start-creations-logging)`, `(set-creations-checkpoint)`, and `(undo-creations)` delete anonymous frames such as `_Situation1` and put the gensym counter back. `(store-kb)` and `(restore-kb)` keep another snapshot on the same value.

`(write-kb)` puts the KM text on `:text`. Two writes of one value match, and the header has no clock. `(save-kb "path")` writes UTF-8. `(reload-kb "path")` resets, then loads. A file whose bytes are not UTF-8 is read as ISO-8859-1. `(load-kb ... :with-morphism ...)` renames symbols for that load. A `(SETQ ...)` of a parameter this port stores is accepted inside the file. The same form at the prompt stays refused. `(fastsave-kb ...)`, `(fastload-kb ...)`, and `(load-newest-kb)` stay refused. On HTTP, `(write-kb)` is 200 and `(fastsave-kb "x.fkm")` is 501. `(a Cat)` stays 501.

The note is `docs/migration/13-load-save-and-undo.md`. The README Done row and `docs/reference/Architecture.md` include `som.km.loadkb` and `som.km.writer`. Nothing is committed. Step 14 is English text and the taxonomy listing, when you want it.

## Assistant

The planned steps are finished, and a large part of the engine already matches Knowledge Machine 2.5.45. What still changes an outcome is concentrated in a few unfinished reasoning paths. A query that Lisp answers with a new instance, a filtered slot, or a reclassified frame comes back here as a refusal, a different value, or a knowledge base with different links. Loading a file that contains one of those forms stops, even when error handling is set to continue.

Notes 00 through 05 describe the engine before a query ran. Later notes close many of those gaps. The open differences below are the ones no later note takes back. Two of those later closures are worth naming so they are not redone: a name lookup now runs with tracing off and restores the flag, and `(load-kb ... :print-statistics t)` prints the real counter line.

## Where an answer or the knowledge base diverges

**Creating an ordinary instance is refused.** `(a Cat)` and `(a Cat with ...)` return "This form is not ported yet" outside prototype mode. `(a Situation)` and `(a Theory)` do create an instance. In Lisp, `(a Cat)` allocates a Skolem, stores `instance-of`, and evaluates the slot paths. Here the refusal spreads:

- A class rule whose value is `(a Cat)` is not recorded as done, and the slot read fails.
- `(the+ Cat with ...)` searches, and a miss ends in that same refusal.
- `&&` does not evaluate a leftover `(a Class)`, so a covered existential stays in the result.
- A description that has slots, such as `('(a Cat with (color (red))) is '(a Animal))`, is refused and leaves nothing behind.
- An action precondition that goes through `(a Thing with ...)` stops the action instead of counting as an unsatisfied precondition.
- `(in-situation (a Cat))` returns before situations mode is turned on. Lisp evaluates the expression, fails the class check, and still turns the mode on.

A file that contains any of these forms fails to load.

**Classification never runs.** `(every Cat has-definition ...)` and the instance form are refused, and `:eval-instances` on load does not classify. A prototype applies only when the instance is already a stored member of that class. Lisp would test the description, possibly reject the instance, and possibly apply a different prototype. After `&` merges two frames, Lisp classifies the survivor. The port leaves the old classes in place, so a later slot read inherits from the wrong class. The classification counters stay at 0 for the same reason.

**Several forms inside a normal rule are refused.** The handler table still returns the not-ported refusal for `(if ... then ... else ...)`, the `allof` / `oneof` / `forall` family, `called` and `uniquely-called`, and `(the Cat color of Fred)`. Lisp evaluates the condition, filters the set, or reads the slot restricted to that class. A rule that uses one of them never produces the slot value. Step 15's absent list does not name `(if ...)`; the handler table does, and a knowledge base of any size uses it.

**Flags that are stored do not change the read.** `(no-inheritance)` is recorded and the read still inherits. `set-filter` is recorded and the filter is not applied. `(sometimes expr)` is not evaluated as a body. A default expression is included again on the next read, and the extra default filter is absent, so a value Lisp would drop stays in the answer. `(sanity-check ...)` answers `(t)` and does not run its body while the sanity flag is unset.

**The same slot can keep a different value.**

- On a supersituation or visible-theory climb, duplicate values keep the later copy. Lisp keeps the earlier copy.
- A repeated goal whose stored residue is not fully evaluated writes nil and deletes the slot. `(Fred has (age ((the age of Fred))))` followed by `(the age of Fred)` removes `age`. Lisp evaluates the residue and can write a real value back.
- The second prototype pass is not run. An appending slot does not merge with `&&` against a prototype already reached through `clone-built-from`. `remove-clone-cycles` is not run, so a cyclic clone leaves a different graph.
- Thing is not added as a superclass when a class does not name one. `(Thing subsumes Cat)` is nil, `(at-most 1 Thing)` does not count that Cat, and `(every Thing)` answers the class frame `(Cat)` rather than the instances. `(the all-subclasses of Thing)` does include the unplaced Cat. After `reset`, built-in superclass links are stored on the frames, so `(the superclasses of Integer)` is non-empty. Lisp leaves `get-vals` of that slot empty until something stores it, and reads the side table instead.
- Pieces of an `&` expression are stored in the situation chosen for the assertion. Lisp stores those pieces in the situation that was current, because it writes the situation outside that call.
- A deletion stores `(<> value)` and does not write the inverse, so the inverse slot still shows the deleted value.
- A top-level query does not turn inverse installation back on. Lisp does. After inverses have been disabled, `(Fred has (loves (Sue)))` leaves `loves-of` empty here and installs it in Lisp. A load then forces inverses on, so a knowledge base that had them off reloads with them on.

**Explanations cite a different record.** Records are stored oldest first. Lisp stores newest first, and the same duplicate check therefore keeps the other record. `(why)` prints that record as a KM rule. A binding copies the bound-away frame's records onto the survivor and also leaves them on the bound-away frame. Lisp deletes that frame. A `remove-subsumers` record whose triple has disappeared is kept. A cached explanation and a `:set` explanation are not stored, so `(why)` reports that nothing was recorded for a value Lisp can explain. `(result-of ...)` and `(precondition-for ...)` print as `(|<location unknown>| ...)`.

**`(justify)` prints a different sentence.** With both flags at their defaults, the port prints the subgoal line. Lisp prints that line only in developer mode. When `justify-leaves` is on and developer mode is off, the sentence is `The <slot> of <phrase> = <phrase>.` and the `(from Class, ...)` suffix is missing, because `originated-from-classes` is unported. `(comment ...)` and `(show-comment ...)` are refused, so the because line and the ENTRY TEXT and EXIT TEXT lines are absent, and a save writes an empty comments section. `(explain-all)` and `(clear-explanations)` are refused.

**Order changes which instance is first.** `(taxonomy)` walks the stored list. Lisp sorts with `string<`. A later superclass of Thing appears earlier, because that slot inserts at the front. The cleanup deletes a redundant link and does not reattach an unplaced class to its partition, so the class stays marked `?` and the subclass links stay unplaced. A save groups frames by insertion order. Lisp sorts each group by symbol name. `(the1 ...)`, `(the2 ...)`, `(the3 ...)`, and `(theNth ...)` are refused, so a query that would read by position fails before the order can matter. `showme` and `showme-all` are refused for the same reason. Two saves of one value match. Lisp includes the time, so two saves of one image differ as text. That determinism is the better file; it is a transcript difference only.

**A round trip moves facts.** A situation-local explanation is written and then stored in `*Global` on reload, so `(why)` after the load looks in the wrong situation. The empty prototype is saved as `(a-prototype Class)`. Lisp writes the misspelled head `a-prototoype` when there are no slot pairs, so that form does not reload on the other side. `(has-definition ...)` and `(in-every-situation ...)` are written when those slots are stored, and both commands are refused, so the file stops. `undo` restores the snapshot, including done flags. Lisp replays the log and then clears those flags. `undo-creations` does not accept a dont-delete list and does not return to `*Global`. `store-kb` keeps the undo history on the value. A binding cycle throws. Lisp loops. The throw is the outcome to keep.

**Smaller command gaps still change a session.** `also-has` and `also-hasnt` on an instance are refused. Class-level `also-has` and `now-has` already append or replace the rule. `do-script`, `in-every-situation`, `nowexists`, `is-true`, `all-true`, and `some-true` are refused. `most-specific`, `most-general`, and a custom aggregation function are refused. `(function ...)` is refused. `fastsave-kb`, `fastload-kb`, `faslsave-kb`, and `load-newest-kb` are refused, and `(SETQ ...)` or `eval` at the prompt is refused. A `.km` file of Knowledge Machine expressions is the load path the plan chose. A fastsave image or a file that splices a Lisp variable with `#t` does not load. `#t` at the prompt and on HTTP looks in an empty map. A file in an encoding other than UTF-8 or ISO-8859-1 can change a string.

**Printed text differs while the answer list stays.** A number below 1 uses a two-digit Java exponent, so `0.0001` is `1.000e-04`. A positive statistics line keeps the extra closing parenthesis from the Lisp format. The access count is one successful `get-vals`, and a top-level query zeroes it, so two knowledge-base values can differ in that counter when the frames are the same. `(license)` prints the repository `LICENSE` file (1994–2015, `peterc@allenai.org`). The Lisp function prints the older `licence.lisp` body. `(pluralize ...)` records the KM 1.4 error and answers nil, which is what 2.5.45 does. `(trace)` records the line and continues. The menu is not read, so a session cannot redo or fail at that point. Finer classify, unify, constraint, and other-situation comments are not printed. A traced form that is not ported yet makes the prompt print the trace and `nil`. A query deeper than 400 stops. Lisp has no default depth ceiling. Queries that fit match.

## What to leave alone

These are already the same outcome, or they are a boundary the plan recorded on purpose.

- One handler table. `compiled-handlers.lisp` and `compiler.lisp` stay out. `htextify` returns nothing, and ordinary `(taxonomy)` does not ask it for HTML.
- No interactive debugger. `continue`, `ignore`, and `abort` already match when the caller does not resume a Lisp restart.
- Saves stay deterministic and omit `(now)`.
- A binding cycle throws.
- `(pluralize)` stays the 2.5.45 error.
- The multiple-next-events sentence keeps the Lisp argument order.
- `(license)` keeps the repository licence.
- `km-equal`, the ratio and overflow arithmetic, and `0 / 0` yielding `(1)` already follow the Lisp.
- Insertion order replaces hash order. Hash order already differs between Lisp implementations. Sorts that Lisp does with `string<` are a separate change, listed below.

## Next steps

Each step is done when the queries in its note return the same answer list and the same frames on both sides, and `clojure -M:test` is green.

1. **Ordinary instances.** Port `(a Class)` and `(a Class with ...)` outside prototype mode, including the Skolem, the object stack, and path evaluation. `(the+ ...)` on a miss, an inherited `(a Cat)`, and a leftover existential in `&&` then return an instance. This is the change that lets a normal knowledge base answer.

2. **Definitions and classification.** Port `has-definition` and `now-has-definition`. Classify on a slot read, after a successful `&`, and when load is asked to evaluate instances. A prototype then applies through that test, including when the description has slots. Count those attempts in the classification counters.

3. **The rule bodies those definitions contain.** Port `(if ... then ... else ...)`, `allof`, `oneof`, `forall` and their `?var in` forms, `called`, `uniquely-called`, and `(the Class slot of frame)`. Creation alone still refuses a typical class rule until these run.

4. **Edits and position.** Port instance `also-has`, `also-hasnt`, and `now-has`, then `(the1)`, `(the2)`, `(the3)`, and `(theNth)`, then sequence `append` and `reverse`.

5. **Make the stored flags affect the read.** `(no-inheritance)` skips inheritance and subslots. `set-filter` filters. `(sometimes ...)` evaluates its body. A default is applied once. A top-level query turns inverse installation back on. Load leaves inverses off when the knowledge base had them off. The inverse of `<>` is written.

6. **One survivor when several sources contribute.** Keep the earlier duplicate on a supersituation or theory climb. Evaluate a looping residue and write it back. Run the second prototype unify for an appending slot, and run `remove-clone-cycles`. Store an `&` assertion's pieces in the current situation, matching the Lisp call. Add Thing only where the Lisp side table would, and leave the built-in superclass slots empty until stored, with the computed walks unchanged.

7. **The same explanation.** Store explanation records newest first, drop a record whose triple is gone, and store a `:set` explanation. Port `(comment ...)`, `(show-comment ...)`, `originated-from-classes`, and the comment-tag walker. `(why)` then prints the because line. `(justify)` adds the from-classes suffix and, with both flags off, omits the developer subgoal line. Port `explain-all` and `clear-explanations`. Put a situation-local explanation back into that situation on load.

8. **The same file.** Accept `a-prototoype` as the empty prototype head, and write it when saving that form. Sort taxonomy and each save group with `string<`, and reattach an unplaced class during `(taxonomy)` cleanup. Reset the inference counters at the start of `load-kb`. After the forms in real files run, a not-ported form inside a load should follow `:on-error` instead of always aborting.

9. **The remaining commands a transcript calls.** `showme`, `showme-all`, `evaluate-all`, `is-true`, `all-true`, `some-true`, `do-script`, `in-every-situation`, `nowexists`, `thelast`, `exists`, `has-value`, and `print` / `format`. `do-script` and `in-every-situation` matter because a save already emits the second of those.

10. **Lock the small texts.** Format a number below 1 with the Lisp exponent width. Leave the extra statistics parenthesis in place, since that is the Lisp sentence. Add `(version)` if a transcript needs the command to return.

The port is wrapped up when a corpus of `.km` files that use creation, definitions, `(if ...)`, and quantifiers loads on both sides, and a fixed set of queries agrees on the answer list under `km-equal`, on which frames are instances of each class, and on `(why)` where the corpus records an explanation. The permanent list above is the residue that comparison is allowed to show.
